\"\"\"Return one deterministic record per physical neural recording.\"\"\"\n    exp_info = np.load(BEH_ROOT / \"Imaging_Exp_info.npy\", allow_pickle=True).item()\n    records: list[dict] = []\n             continue\n            seen.add(sid)\n            behavior_key = sid\n            if \"stimtype\" in db:\n                behavior_key = f\"{sid}_{db['stimtype']}\"\n            if \"days\" in db:\n                day_value = float(db[\"days\"])\n                day_source = \"days\"\n            elif \"sess#\" in db:\n                day_value = float(db[\"sess#\"])\n                day_source = \"sess#\"\n            else:\n                raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: neither 'days' nor 'sess#' is available\")\n            records.append(\n                {\n                    \"session_id\": sid,\n                    \"group\": group,\n                    \"behavior_key\": behavior_key,\n                    \"db\": db,\n                    \"day_value\": day_value,\n                    \"day_source\": day_source,\n                }\n            )\n    if len(records) != 89:\n        raise AssertionError(f\"Expected 89 physical sessions, found {len(records)}\")\n    return records\n\n\ndef records_by_group(records: list[dict]) -> OrderedDict[str, list[dict]]:\n    grouped: OrderedDict[str, list[dict]] = OrderedDict()\n    for record in records:\n        grouped.setdefault(record[\"group\"], []).append(record)\n    return grouped\n\n\ndef get_behavior(behavior_dict: dict, record: dict) -> dict:\n    key = record[\"behavior_key\"]\n    if key in behavior_dict:\n        return behavior_dict[key]\n    matches = [k for k in behavior_dict if k.startswith(record[\"session_id\"])]\n    if len(matches) != 1:\n        raise KeyError(\n            f\"{record['session_id']}: expected one behavior key, got {matches}\"\n        )\n    record[\"behavior_key\"] = matches[0]\n    return behavior_dict[matches[0]]\n\n\ndef validate_behavior_shapes(beh: \"SoundTime\", \"RewTime\", \"RewPos\", \"isRew\", \"WallName\",\n        \"RewardFr\", \"StartFr\", \"GrayFr\", \"EndFr\", \"SoundFr\",\n    ]\n    frame_fields = [\n        \"ft\", \"ft_trInd\", {field} length {len(beh[field])} != ntrials {ntrials}\"\n            )\n    nframes = len(beh[\"ft\"])\n    for field in frame_fields:\n        if len(beh[field]) != nframes:\n            raise ValueError(\n                f\"{sid}: {field} length {len(beh[field])} != ft length {nframes}\"\n            )\n    if tuple(np.asarray(beh[\"run_pos\"]).shape) != (ntrials, 60):\n        raise        and float(beh[\"Gray_Space_length\"]) == 20.0\n    ):\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: unexpected corridor geometry\")\n\n\ndef selected_frames_by_trial(beh: dict, sid: str) -> list[np.ndarray]:\n    \"\"\"Apply the paper's moving, textured-corridor frame mask.\"\"\"\n    trial_id = np.asarray(beh[\"ft_trInd\"])\n    valid = (\n        np.isfinite(trial_id)\n        & np.asarray(beh[\"ft_CorrSpc\"], dtype=bool)\n        & (np.asarray(beh[\"ft_move\"]) > 0)\n    )\n    selected = np.flatnonzero(valid)\n    selected_trial = trial_id[selected].astype(np.int64)\n    ntrials = int(beh[\"ntrials\"])\n    if np.any((selected_trial < 0) | (selected_trial >= ntrials)):\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: selected frames contain invalid trial IDs\")\n    frames = [selected[selected_trial == trial] for trial in range(ntrials)]\n    lengths = np.asarray([len(x) for x in frames])\n    if np.any(lengths < 2):\n        bad = np.flatnonzero(lengths < 2).tolist()\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: trials with fewer than two valid frames: {bad}\")\n    positions = np.asarray(beh[\"ft_Pos\"])[selected]\n    if not np.all(np.isfinite(positions)) or np.any((positions < 0) | (positions >= 40)):\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: selected positions are outside [0, 40)\")\n    speed = np.asarray(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"])[selected]\n    if not np.all(np.isfinite(speed)):\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: selected running speed contains NaN/Inf\")\n    return frames\n\n\ndef speed_quartile_prepass(records: list[dict]) -> tuple[np.ndarray, dict]:\n    \"\"\"Validate behavior and get global speed quartile edges.\"\"\"\n    t0 = time.perf_counter()\n    speed_chunks: list[np.ndarray] = []\n    trial_lengths: list[int] = []\n    total_trials = 0\n    rewarded_deliveries = 0\n    rewarded_availability = 0\n    for group, group_records in records_by_group(records).items():\n        behavior_dict = np.load(\n            BEH_ROOT / f\"Beh_{group}.npy\", allow_pickle=True\n        ).item()\n        for record in group_records:\n            beh = get_behavior(behavior_dict, record)\n            sid = record[\"session_id\"]\n            validate_behavior_shapes(beh, sid)\n            frames = selected_frames_by_trial(beh, sid)\n            for idx in frames:\n                speed_chunks.append(np.asarray(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"])[idx])\n                trial_lengths.append(len(idx))\n            total_trials += int(beh[\"ntrials\"])\n            is_rewarded = np.asarray(beh[\"isRew\"], dtype=bool)\n            rewarded_deliveries += int(is_rewarded.sum())\n            reward_walls = np.unique(np.asarray(beh[\"WallName\"])[is_rewarded])\n            if len(reward_walls) > 1:\n                raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: multiple rewarded walls {reward_walls.tolist()}\")\n            if len(reward_walls) == 1:\n                rewarded_availability += int(\n                    np.sum(np.asarray(beh[\"WallName\"]) == reward_walls[0])\n                )\n        del behavior_dict\n        gc.collect()\n    speeds = np.concatenate(speed_chunks).astype(np.float64, copy=False)\n    thresholds = np.quantile(speeds, [0.25, 0.50, 0.75])\n    if not np.all(np.diff(thresholds) > 0):\n        raise ValueError(f\"Non-unique running-speed quartile thresholds: {thresholds}\")\n    lengths = np.asarray(trial_lengths)\n    summary = {\n        \"n_trials\": int(total_trials),\n        \"n_timepoints\": int(len(speeds)),\n        \"trial_length_min\": int(lengths.min()),\n        \"trial_length_mean\": float(lengths.mean()),\n        \"trial_length_median\": float(np.median(lengths)),\n        \"trial_length_max\": int(lengths.max()),\n        \"speed_min\": float(speeds.min()),\n        \"speed_max\": float(speeds.max()),\n        \"reward_deliveries\": int(rewarded_deliveries),\n        \"reward_availability_trials\": int(rewarded_availability),\n    }\n    print(\n        f\"Behavior prepass: {total_trials:,} trials, {len(speeds):,} selected \"\n        f\"timepoints, speed edges {thresholds.tolist()} \"\n        f\"({time.perf_counter() - t0:.2f} s)\",\n        flush=True,\n    )\n    return thresholds, summary\n\n\ndef raw_area_to_region_idx(iarea: np.ndarray, sid: str) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n    \"\"\"Return named-area keep mask and target indices, matching reference code.\"\"\"\n    iarea = np.asarray(iarea).astype(np.int64, copy=False)\n    region = np.full(len(iarea), -1, dtype=np.int16)\n    region[iarea == 8] = 0\n    region[np.isin(iarea, [0, 1, 2, 9])] = 1\n    region[np.isin(iarea, [5, 6])] = 2\n    region[np.isin(iarea, [3, 4])] = 3\n    keep = region >= 0\n    unexpected = np.unique(iarea[(~keep) & (~np.isin(iarea, [-1, 7]))])\n    if len(unexpected):\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: unmapped retinotopy codes {unexpected.tolist()}\")\n    if not np.any(keep):\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: no cells in named visual regions\")\n    return keep, region[keep]\n\n\ndef load_filtered_neural(record: dict) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n    \"\"\"Load one neural session and retain named visual-area cells.\"\"\"\n    sid = record[\"session_id\"]\n    db = record[\"db\"]\n    ret_path = RET_ROOT / f\"{db['mname']}_{db['datexp']}_trans.npz\"\n    with np.load(ret_path, allow_pickle=True) as ret:\n        iarea = np.asarray(ret[\"iarea\"])\n    keep, region_idx = raw_area_to_region_idx(iarea, sid)\n\n    spk_path = SPK_ROOT / f\"{sid}_neural_data.npy\"\n    raw = np.load(spk_path, allow_pickle=True).item()\n    if set(raw) != {\"spks\"} or not isinstance(raw[\"spks\"], list):\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: unexpected neural file structure\")\n    planes = raw[\"spks\"]\n    if not planes:\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: empty spks list\")\n    nframes_set = {int(x.shape[1]) for x in planes}\n    if len(nframes_set) != 1:\n        raise ValueError(f\"{sid}: imaging planes have different frame counts\")\n    nframes = nframes_set.pop()\n    raw_neurons = sum(int(x.shape[0]) for x in planes)\n    if raw_neurons != len(iarea):\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"{sid}: spks has {raw_neurons} cells but retinotopy has {len(iarea)}\"\n        )\n\n    # Fill the retained matrix directly to avoid concatenating every raw cell first.\n    activity = np.empty((int(keep.sum()), nframes), dtype=np.float32)\n    raw_offset = 0\n    kept_offset = 0\n    for plane in planes:\n        nplane = int(plane.shape[0])\n        plane_keep = keep[raw_offset : raw_offset + nplane]\n A recording listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen, leaving 89 sessions.\n\nii. The recording sometimes repeat under different behavior session type. Therefore a recording that is listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n\nii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n```python\nframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n```\n\nand the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n\niii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n\nii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n```python\ntrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n```\n```python\nlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\nreturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n```\n\niii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. The visual area of each neuron comes from `iarea` in `retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`.\n\nii. The two reads and the concatenation:\n```python\nplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\nspikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\narea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n```\n\niii. The neural data is read directly, nothing is derived from it.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The traces are not processed. The columns belonging to a trial are taken and the result is stored as float16. The trials are variable length and are left that way, so nothing is padded.\n\nii. One trial of neural data:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The file already holds deconvolved traces, which the methods state every analysis was based on, so no dF/F or deconvolution is needed. float16 rather than float32 because the dataset is quite big. \n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n\nii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n```python\nregion = np.full(area.size, -1)\nfor index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\nkeep = region >= 0\nreturn spikes[keep], region[keep]\n```\n\niii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n\nii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. It is not binned or resampled. The imaging frames are the bins: one column of `spks` per frame at 3.17 Hz, so a bin is 315 ms, and a trial holds as many of them as its traversal lasted, a median of 23.\n\nii. The rate, and the bin size it gives the metadata:\n```python\nFRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size': 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE,        # ms\n```\n\niii. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr`, the frame at which the sound cue was played in each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n\nii. The frame times of the session, and the cue of every trial placed on them:\n```python\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\ncue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\niii. The timing is dervied from the frame number.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_to_sound_cue*?\n\ni. Note that here the instruction as *time_to_sound_cue*, so it's positive before the sound cue, and negative after the sound cue.\n\nii. The day is how many recorded days the mouse is into training, so its first session is 0 and each later one counts up, to at most 7. Note that this is *time_since_trial_start* so it is negative before start, postive after.\n\nii. The input built from the trial time axis:\n```python\ntime - start[trial]\n```\n\niii. Source field/group stored in session metadata. |\n| `Trial_start_time`, `ft` | `input[2]` | `(ft[selected_frames] - Trial_start_time[trial]) * 86400`, seconds since corridor entry; float32 | Frame alignment documented in notebook | Preserves actual elapsed time across omitted stationary frames. |\n| `WallName`, `isRew` | `input[3]` | Identify the unique rewarded wall from `WallName[isRew]`; mark all trials of that wall 1, others 0; repeat across frames; float32 | `get_cat_id` similarly identifies rewarded stimulus by wall | Encodes reward *availability*, not delivery. No-reward sessions are all 0. |\n| `WallName` prefix | `output[0]` | Map `circle*→0`, `leaf*→1`, `rock*→2`, `wood*→3`; repeat trial category across frames; int16 | Paper's category definitions; native `WallName` | Exemplar number and swap do not change visual category. Values: circle, leaf, rock, wood (paper calls the last image family brick). |\n| `LickFr`, `LickTrind` | `output[1]` | Reference-style integer/floor frame assignment; 1 where at least one lick from that trial lands on selected frame, else 0; int16 | `spk_2_firstLick`, `spk_2_cue` cast `LickFr.astype(int)` | Binary even if multiple licks occupy one frame. Licks during excluded stationary/gray frames are outside the converted interval. |\n| `ft_Pos` | `output[2]` | `floor(position_source_units / 10)` for selected 0–40 source-unit frames; clip defensively to 0–3; int16 | `get_interpPos_spk` establishes 10 source units = 1 m | Four equal physical bins: 0–1, 1–2, 2–3, 3–4 m. Direct `ft_Pos` avoids cumulative-position offsets observed in 21 boundary samples. |\n| `ft_RunSpeed` | `output[3]` | Compute 25th/50th/75th percentiles over all selected timepoints in the converted session set; `np.digitize` into 0–3; int16 | Paper running-speed section uses imaging-frame interpolation; notebook defines `ft_RunSpeed` | Full-data preliminary edges: about 12.4224, 25.3526, 40.8546 (native speed units); script recomputes exact edges for full/sample modes and stores them. |\n| retinotopy `iarea` | `brain_region_idx` and neural row filter | Keep codes other than −1,7; map 8→V1, {0,1,2,9}→mHV, {5,6}→lHV, {3,4}→aHV | `neu_area_ID`; `Get_density_map` | `brain_regions = ['V1','mHV','lHV','aHV']`; row order remains exactly the released concatenated cell order. |\n| mouse name in experiment descriptor | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Deterministic subject list and per-session integer lookup | Experiment-index loading | Sample mode lists only subjects present in its two sessions; full mode contains all 19. |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Session identity**: Use the first experiment-index occurrence of each mouse/date/block and convert 89 physical recordings. Repeated group labels and swap annotations do not create new neural sessions.\n2. **Temporal representation**: Preserve original imaging-frame activity rather than spatially interpolating to 60 bins. The target requires a common time-bin duration and temporal cue/lick variables; spatial interpolation would replace time with position. The nominal bin is `1000/3.17 = 315.4574 ms`, while actual elapsed times are carried explicitly because excluded stationary frames create gaps.\n3. **Reference running restriction**: Retain only advancing-VR textured-corridor frames, matching all main neural-response analyses and preventing reward-consumption pauses from dominating. Preliminary audit: 821,579 selected observations, 11–178 per trial (median 21), with every trial retained.\n4. **Neural values**: Preserve float32 deconvolved activity without normalization. Reference d-prime uses raw deconvolved frames; decoder code learns per-session projections and does not require conversion-time z-scoring.\n5. **Area curation**: Exclude raw area −1/7 cells because the reference says they are outside visual cortex. No stimulus-selectivity filtering is applied: d-prime thresholds are analysis-specific and would leak the requested visual output into neuron selection.\n6. **Trial curation**: Keep every imaging trial because all have valid aligned running/corridor frames and all sessions have at least 84 trials. Behavior-only sessions are excluded solely because they lack neural input.\n7. **Mixed variable shapes**: Store all inputs as float32 `(4,T)` and outputs as integer `(4,T)`, broadcasting per-trial day/reward/category. A single trial array cannot otherwise combine scalar and time-varying dimensions, and this is the validator/trainer's documented convention.\n8. **Speed binning**: Use global selected-observation quartiles, not per-session quartiles, so labels have a consistent meaning for the shared decoder and each class represents approximately 25% of full data.\n9. **Metadata**: `off_start=0.0` because conceptual trial start is corridor entry; `off_end=None` because elapsed end time varies. Document moving-frame selection/gaps, speed edges, full native/retained counts, category mapping, and per-session source details.\n10. **Storage/efficiency**: Load/process one multi-GB neural session at a time; filter planes before concatenation; use float32 neural/input and compact integer outputs; release raw arrays immediately. Behavior prepass is small and computes global speed thresholds before neural I/O.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Structural: 89 unique full sessions (2 sample), aligned neural/input/output trial counts, every trial `T>=2`, constant neurons within session, no NaN/Inf.\n- [ ] Native neural spot-check: load a raw neural file independently and use `np.allclose` on specific retained cells/frames versus converted trials.\n- [ ] Native input spot-check: independently recompute cue time, training day, elapsed time, and reward availability for specific frames and compare with `np.allclose`.\n- [ ] Native output spot-check: independently recompute category, lick event, 1-m position bin, and quartile speed bin for specific frames and compare with `np.allclose`.\n- [ ] Alignment: selected raw indices exactly satisfy trial ID, textured-corridor, and movement masks; plot neural/activity plus cue, lick, position, speed after alignment.\n- [ ] Curation: raw cells = 4,691,034; retained named-area cells = 4,105,393; per-session raw range matches paper 20,547–89,577.\n- [ ] Dataset size: match paper's 89 recordings/19 mice and native 38,110 trials; no physical session duplicated or lost.\n- [ ] Outputs: category/lick ranges valid; position bins cover 0–3 and correspond exactly to meter intervals; speed class fractions each approximately 0.25.\n- [ ] Reward: availability positives equal all trials of the identified rewarded wall (preliminary 4,446), exceeding delivered rewards (4,336) only in active sessions as expected.\n- [ ] Reference comparison: raw imaging-frame values (not spatial interpolation) and movement/corridor masks match the applicable reference functions; all deliberate differences documented.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n[Implemented `/app/convert_data.py` with the required positional output path, default `--full`, `--sample`, and `--show-processing`. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `/app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `/app/verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `/app/README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `/app/cache/` folder\\n- Create `/app/cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. \\\\'\\\\'\\\\'find x >= xmin and <= xmax\\\\'\\\\'\\\\'\\\\n    return (x>=xmin) & (x<=xmax)\\\\n\\\\ndef get_cat_id(WallName, isRew):\\\\n    uniqW = np.unique(WallName)\\\\n    rewStim =                 FLTr.append(n)\\\\n\\\\n            LickRaster[typ][tw][\\\\'LickPos\\\\'] = LPos\\\\n            LickRaster[typ][tw][\\\\'LickTr\\\\'] = LTr\\\\n        FL = pretrain_exp_lick_raster(dat[ky])[\\\\'firstLick\\\\'][0][0]\\\\n        count,_ = np.histogram(FL, range=[0,40], bins=40)\\\\n        hist.append(count/len(FL))\\\\n    hist = np.array(hist)\\\\n    return hist.reshape(int(hist.shape[0]/5), 5, 40)\\\\n\\\\ndef lickCount(dats, def_range=[]):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"return binary lick response for each trial\\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    befRew, aftRew, inRange = [], [], []\\\\n    for n in range(dats[\\\\'ntrials\\\\']):\\\\n        RewTime, SoundTime = dats[\\\\'RewTime\\\\'], dats[\\\\'SoundTime\\\\']\\\\n        cue_del = SoundTime + dats[\\\\'Reward_Delay_ms\\\\']/(1000*3600*24) # convert to datenum \\\\n        if dats[\\\\'Reward_Mode\\\\']==\\\\'Passive\\\\':\\\\n            befRew.append(find_between(dats[\\\\'LickTime\\\\'],dats[\\\\'Trial_start_time\\\\'][n],cue_del[n]))\\\\n        elif dats[\\\\'Reward_Mode\\\\']==\\\\'Active after cue\\\\':\\\\n            befRew.append(find_between(dats[\\\\'LickTime\\\\'],dats[\\\\'Trial_start_time\\\\'][n],dats[\\\\'Gray_space_time\\\\'][n]))\\\\n        aftRew.append(find_between(dats[\\\\'LickTime\\\\'],RewTime[n],dats[\\\\'Gray_space_time\\\\'][n]))\\\\n        if len(def_range)==2:\\\\n            inRange.append(find_between(dats[\\\\'LickPos\\\\'], def_range[0], def_range[1]) & (dats[\\\\'LickTrind\\\\']==n))\\\\n        else:\\\\n            inRange.append([np.nan])\\\\n\\\\n    lickResp = lickResp\\\\n\\\\ndef lick_response(dats, trial_include=[], def_range=[]):\\\\n    lickResp= lickCount(dats, def_range=def_range)\\\\n    \\\\n    Resp = {}\\\\n    Resp[\\\\'tex_name\\\\'] =    ntrials = dats[\\\\'ntrials\\\\']\\\\n    trial_include = np.array(trial_include)\\\\n\\\\n    kn = list(lickResp.keys())\\\\n    for w,wn in enumerate(Resp[\\\\'tex_name\\\\']):\\\\n        Resp[wn] = {}\\\\n        for k in range(len(kn)):\\\\n            idx = dats[\\\\'WallName\\\\']==wn\\\\n            trial_n = np.arange(ntrials)\\\\n            if len(trial_include)==2:\\\\n                idx = idx & (trial_n>=trial_include[0]) & (trial_n<=trial_include[1])\\\\n\\\\n            Resp[wn][kn[k]] = np.array([np.mean(lickResp[kn[k]][idx]), \\\\n                                        np.std(lickResp[kn[k]][idx], ddof=1)/np.sqrt(idx.sum())])\\\\n    return Resp\\\\t\\\\n\\\\ndef get_mean_lick_response(beh, lick_typ=\\\\'befRew\\\\'):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"lick_typ: befRew, aftRew x2):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"x1,x2: neurons * frames \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    u1, u2 = np.nanmean(x1, 1), np.nanmean(x2, 1)\\\\n    sig1, sig2 = np.nanstd(x1, 1), np.nanstd(x2, 1)\\\\n    return 2 * (u1 - u2) / (sig1 + sig2)      \\\\n\\\\ndef get_dist_img(xpos, ypos, sel_idx=None, sig=30, sig_bg=30, xoff=800, yoff=800):\\\\n    from scipy import ndimage\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"create an binary image for indicate neurons\\\\' position using xpos and ypos\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    x, y = xpos+xoff, ypos+yoff\\\\n    bg, img = np.zeros((5000,5000)), np.zeros((5000,5000))\\\\n    bg[y.astype(int), x.astype(int)] = 1.0\\\\n    bg = ndimage.gaussian_filter(bg, sigma=1, truncate=sig_bg)>0 # create a background map\\\\n    bg = bg.astype(float)\\\\n    bg[bg==False] = np.nan # fill empty space with nan\\\\n    \\\\n    if any(sel_idx != None):\\\\n        img[y[sel_idx].astype(int), x[sel_idx].astype(int)] = 1\\\\n    img = ndimage.gaussian_filter(img, sigma=sig)\\\\n    img[np.isnan(bg)] = np.nan\\\\n    img = np.fliplr(img)\\\\n    return img    \\\\n\\\\ndef Get_density_map(dprime, retinotopy, dp_thr=0.3, typ=\\\\'both\\\\', n_down = 10):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"dp_thr: threshold,\\\\n    n_down: down sample ratio\\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    xoff, yoff =800,800   \\\\n    img = []\\\\n    for i in range(len(dprime)):   \\\\n        ixy, arid = retinotopy[i][\\\\'xy_t\\\\'], retinotopy[i][\\\\'iarea\\\\']\\\\n        dp = dprime[i]\\\\n        if typ==\\\\'both\\\\':\\\\n            idx = abs(dp) >= dp_thr\\\\n        elif typ==\\\\'stim1\\\\':\\\\n            idx = dp >= dp_thr\\\\n        elif typ==\\\\'stim2\\\\':\\\\n            idx = dp <= -dp_thr            \\\\n        idx_neu = (arid!=-1) & (arid != 7) # exclude neurons from outside of visual cortex\\\\n        nneu = idx_neu.sum() # total neurons\\\\n        xpos, ypos = -ixy[idx_neu, 1], ixy[idx_neu, 0]\\\\n\\\\n        img0 = get_dist_img(xpos, ypos, idx[idx_neu], xoff=xoff, yoff=yoff)\\\\n        img.append(img0/nneu) # devided by total neurons\\\\n    img = np.nanmean(np.array(img), axis=0) # mean across mice\\\\n    imgs = {\\\\'img\\\\':img[::n_down, ::n_down], \\\\'n_down\\\\':n_down} # No reference function computes \\u0394F/F from fluorescence, so no new \\u0394F/F calculation is warranted.\\\\n+- Reference temporal alignment uses behavior variables already sampled/aligned to neural frames (`ft*`, `StartFr`, `EndFr`, `SoundFr`, `LickFr`, etc.). The spatial-analysis interpolation first retains `ft_move > 0`, then maps activity against `ft_PosCum` into 60 equally spaced 0.1 m bins across the full 6 m trial (4 m textured corridor plus 2 m gray space).\\\\n+- Paper analyses sometimes restrict to moving textured-corridor frames and sometimes select stimulus-responsive neurons. Those restrictions support particular figure analyses; their applicability to a general neural decoder will be decided after reconciling the provided decoder specification with the raw variables and methods.\\\\n+- Area curation in density-map analyses excludes raw codes `-1` and `7` as outside visual cortex. The named-region mapping itself covers raw codes V1=8, mHV={0,1,2,9}, lHV={5,6}, aHV={3,4}; unmapped codes will require explicit handling after dataset inspection.\\\\n+- No generic cell-quality threshold or electrophysiology quality filtering appears: this is two-photon calcium imaging, not electrophysiology. Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as \\u2018frozen\\u2019 crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as \\u2018leaf\\u2019 and \\u2018circle\\u2019, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (\\u2018rock\\u2019 and \\u2018bricks\\u2019; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli \\u2018leaf2\\u2019 and \\u2018circle2\\u2019, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (\\u2018leaf3\\u2019) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\\\\n\\\\nMice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\\\\n\\\\nBefore and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\\\\n\\\\nIt is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \\\\n\\\\nMethods\\\\n\\\\nWe performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s \\u00d7 camK2a-tTa mice.\\\\n\\\\nThe mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\\\\n\\\\nVisual stimuli\\\\n\\\\nWe showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270\\u00b0 of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4\\\\u2009m long, with 2\\\\u2009m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s\\u22121, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s\\u22121) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\\\\n\\\\nThe virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0\\u00b0 and 45\\u00b0) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (\\u2018unsupervised gratings\\u2019 and VRg mice).\\\\n\\\\nFor behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\\\\n\\\\nFor imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\\\\n\\\\nThe two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\\\\n\\\\nBehavioural training\\\\n\\\\nAll animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5\\u20131\\\\u2009h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1\\\\u2009h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality\\\\u2009+\\\\u2009reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\\\\n\\\\nFor the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality\\\\u2009+\\\\u2009reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2\\\\u2009m and 3\\\\u2009m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2\\u20133\\\\u2009m.\\\\n\\\\nFor imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5\\\\u2009m and 3.5\\\\u2009m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5\\\\u2009m and 3.5\\\\u2009m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\\\\n\\\\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\\\\n\\\\nCalcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Suite2p performs motion correction, region of interest detection, cell classification, neuropil correction and spike deconvolution as previously described64. For non-negative deconvolution, we used a timescale of decay of 0.75\\\\u2009s (ref. 65). All our analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces.\\\\n'}]\"\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:495:        \"raw_arguments\": \"const patch = \\\"*** Begin Patch\\\\n*** Update File: /app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md\\\\n@@\\\\n ## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\\\\n-**Status**: IN PROGRESS\\\\n+**Status**: COMPLETE\\\\n@@\\\\n ### Data Structure\\\\n-[Describe file organization]\\\\n+- Total data footprint: approximately 412 GB apparent size.\\\\n+- `data/beh/` (28 files, 6.6 GB): `Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is a dictionary with 23 experiment-group keys and 142 group/session descriptors. Despite all these differences, \\\\nneural plasticity was similar to the unsupervised cohort, suggesting \\\\nthat most of the plasticity in these areas relates to the stimuli directly, \\\\nirrespective of other factors.\\\\nVisual, not spatial, representations\\\\nIt is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spa-\\\\ntial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate \\\\nfiring rates even in the visual cortex36. Alternatively, the neural plasticity \\\\nmight be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images \\\\nthat we presented14. T o distinguish between a spatial and a visual plastic-\\\\nity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 \\\\nand circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, \\\\nrespectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations \\\\n(Fig.\\\\xa02a). We found that mice only licked to leaf2 and not to circle2, prob-\\\\nably due to their visual similarities with the trained stimuli (Fig.\\\\xa02b,c).\\\\nThe spatial plasticity hypothesis suggests that neurons would \\\\nfire in a similar sequence to learned and new exemplars of the same \\\\ncategory. We tested this directly by sorting neurons according to their \\\\nsequence of firing in the leaf1 corridor. This sorting did not induce \\\\nsimilar sequences for the leaf2 corridor in the medial regions (Fig.\\\\xa02d). \\\\nThe neural sequences in leaf1 and leaf2 were in fact uncorrelated in all \\\\nregions, suggesting a non-spatial coding scheme (Fig.\\\\xa02e,f). Further-\\\\nmore, the sequence correlations did not match the behavioural strategy \\\\nof the mice (Fig.\\\\xa02c,e). Similar results were found when analysing the \\\\nsequences of the circle1-selective neurons (Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa03a,b).\\\\nThe visual plasticity hypothesis suggests that the statistics of visual \\\\nfeatures (that is, \\u2018leafiness\\u2019) are learned regardless of where the features \\\\noccur in the corridor. T o test this, we designed an analysis that uses \\\\nthe top selective neurons of each familiar corridor to create a coding \\\\ndirection axis37 (Fig.\\\\xa02g,h). Projections of neural data on the coding \\\\ndirection were well separated on test trials of the familiar stimuli, and \\\\nalso on trials of the new leaf2 and circle2 stimuli (Fig.\\\\xa02i and Extended \\\\nData Fig.\\\\xa03c\\u2013e). T o quantify this separation, we defined a similarity \\\\nindex computed from the projections on the coding axis. The similar-\\\\nity index clearly distinguished between corridors of different visual \\\\ncategories, in all visual regions, in both task and unsupervised mice \\\\nas well as in naive mice (Fig.\\\\xa02j). Thus, the coding direction readout of \\\\nneural activity matched the behavioural strategy of the mice (Fig.\\\\xa02c,i), \\\\nsuggesting that the brain areas that we considered use visual rather However, the coding direction readout did not directly \\\\nreflect licking behaviour because it stayed unchanged between trials \\\\nwith early cues, which contained only a few anticipatory licks, and trials \\\\nwith late cues, which contained many licks (Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa04a\\u2013c). \\\\nAn exception to this pattern was, again, the anterior region on leaf1 \\\\ntrials (see below for the likely reason).\\\\nNovelty and orthogonalization\\\\nAlthough the neurons selective to familiar stimuli also responded to \\\\nthe new stimuli, they were by far not the most responsive neurons to \\\\nthese new stimuli. Selecting neurons by d\\u2032 between leaf2 and circle1, we \\\\nfound a large population of leaf2-selective neurons in V1 and the lateral \\\\nvisual areas (Fig.\\\\xa03a; see also Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa05a for circle2-selective  \\\\nneurons). The number of leaf2-selective neurons decreased substan-\\\\ntially after an additional week of training with the leaf2 stimulus, sug-\\\\ngesting adaptation (Fig.\\\\xa03a,b). By contrast, the number of leaf1-selective \\\\nneurons showed little or no change after this additional learning phase \\\\n(Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa05b). Thus, V1 and lateral HVAs appear to be respond-\\\\ning to the stimulus novelty, as reported by some previous studies in V1 \\\\n(ref. 38), and the responsive neurons were similarly distributed across \\\\nbrain areas in the task and unsupervised cohorts.\\\\nThe task mice eventually stopped licking to leaf2 (Fig.\\\\xa0 3c), thus \\\\nshowing strong discrimination between leaf1 (rewarded) and leaf2 \\\\n(unrewarded). We hypothesized that this behaviour may be accompa-\\\\nnied by changes in neural discrimination, similar to the changes after \\\\nmice learned the distinction between leaf1 and circle1 (Fig.\\\\xa01j). T o test \\\\nthis, we selected neurons based on their d\\u2032 between leaf1 and leaf2, \\\\nand compared the fraction of tuned neurons across areas in naive mice \\\\nas well as in the task and unsupervised cohorts. Again, we observed a \\\\nsubstantial increase in selectivity in the medial HVAs, in both the task \\\\nand the unsupervised cohorts, but not in the group exposed to the \\\\nvirtual reality gratings (Fig.\\\\xa03d,e).\\\\nBecause leaf1 and leaf2 neural representations were similar in naive \\\\nmice (Fig.\\\\xa02j), we hypothesized that the fine behavioural discrimination \\\\nbetween leaf1 and leaf2 stimuli requires orthogonalization of their \\\\nneural representations28,35. We tested this by comparing the projections \\\\nof leaf2 onto the leaf1\\u2013circle1 coding direction (Fig.\\\\xa03f,g). Compared \\\\nwith naive mice, this projection was reduced after both supervised \\\\nand unsupervised training, across all visual regions but most strongly \\\\nin the medial HVAs (Fig.\\\\xa03h). There was no change in the control group \\\\ntrained on the virtual reality gratings compared with naive mice. Thus, \\\\nthe responses to leaf2 became orthogonal to the leaf1\\u2013circle1 axis, even \\\\nin the unsupervised mice in which leaf1 and leaf2 had the same valence. \\\\nT ogether, these observations describe the complex dynamics of the \\\\nrepresentation of a new stimulus (leaf2) as it becomes familiar, in both \\\\nsupervised and unsupervised conditions.\\\\na b We hypothesized that a more detailed representation \\\\nof leaf1 emerged to support the visual recognition memory of this \\\\nstimulus. T o test this, we first introduced a new exemplar of the leaf \\\\ncategory (\\u2018leaf3\\u2019; Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa06a). Mice withheld licking to this \\\\nstimulus, similar to their behaviour on the unrewarded, trained leaf2 \\\\nstimulus (Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa06b). This behavioural choice mirrored a \\\\nchange in neural tuning properties: the neural projections of leaf3 trials \\\\nonto the coding axis of leaf1\\u2013leaf2 were strongly biased towards the \\\\nleaf2 direction (Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa06c). This asymmetry was present \\\\nin both the task and the unsupervised cohorts, and it was not present \\\\nin naive mice (Extended Data Fig.\\\\xa06d,e). All visual regio\\u20268446 tokens truncated\\u2026 timescale of decay of 0.75\\\\u2009s (ref. 65). All our analyses \\\\nwere based on deconvolved fluorescence traces.\\\\nNeural selectivity (d\\u2032). T o compute the selectivity index d\\u2032, illustrated \\\\nin Fig.\\\\xa01f, we only selected data points inside the 0\\u20134-m region of the \\\\ncorridors where the textures were shown. We excluded the data points \\\\nin which the animal was not running, so that all data points included \\\\nfor calculating the selectivity index came from similar engagement or \\\\narousal levels of the mice. Note that these data points are computed \\\\nfrom original estimated deconvolved traces without interpolation.  \\\\nWe first calculated the means (\\u03bc 1 and \\u03bc 2) and standard deviations  \\\\n(\\u03c31 and \\u03c32) of activities for any two corridors, then computed the d\\u2032. The \\\\ncriteria for selective neurons was d\\u2032 \\u22650 .3:\\\\nd\\\\n\\u03bc\\u03bc\\u2032 =\\\\n\\u2212\\\\n+\\\\n\\u03c3\\u03c3\\\\n12\\\\n22\\\\n12\\\\nT o make the density plots across the cortex (for example, Fig.\\\\xa01i), we \\\\ncomputed 2D histograms for each session based on the selective neu-\\\\nrons in that session. We then applied a 2D Gaussian filter to this matrix \\\\nand divided by the number of total recorded neurons in that session to \\\\nget a density map for each mouse. Before averaging the density maps \\\\nacross mice, we assigned NaN to areas where no neurons were recorded. \\\\nThis ensured no underestimation on the density within areas where not \\\\nall mice have neurons recorded.\\\\nFor sequence similarity analyses (Fig.\\\\xa02 and Extended Data Figs.\\\\xa03 \\\\nand\\\\xa06), we used half of leaf1 and circle1 trials (train trials) to compute \\\\nthe selectivity index d\\u2032 and we selected neurons based on the criteria \\\\nd\\u2032 \\u22650 .3. We then split the other half of leaf1 and circle1 trials (test  \\\\ntrials) into odd versus even trials to compute spatial tuning curves for \\\\nodd and even trials separately for each selective neuron. From these \\\\nspatial tuning curves, we used the position with the maximal response \\\\nas the preferred position for each neuron. T o compute tuning curves \\\\nfor other stimuli such as leaf2, circle2 and swap (which were not used \\\\nto find selective neurons), we split all trials into odd and even trials. The \\\\npreferred positions of the same neurons in different corridors or in odd \\\\nversus even trials were used to compute a correlation coefficient (r).\\\\nCoding direction and similarity index. Reward-prediction neurons were either \\\\nselected using the clustering algorithm Rastermap (Fig.\\\\xa04a\\u2013d) or using \\\\na d\\u2032 criterion (Fig.\\\\xa04e\\u2013n). Using Rastermap, we selected the reward- \\\\nprediction neurons based on their special firing patterns of only  \\\\nresponding inside the rewarded corridor and specifically before reward \\\\ndelivery. Using d\\u2032, we first interpolated the neural activity of single \\\\nneurons based on their position inside the corridor and constructed a \\\\nmatrix (trials by positions). We used cue position instead \\\\nof reward position because the sound cues were played in each corridor \\\\nat a random position, with or without reward, and these sound cue \\\\npositions were highly correlated with reward positions in the rewarded \\\\ncorridor (Fig.\\\\xa01c). The \\\\nactivity of the reward-prediction neural population in Fig.\\\\xa04 (except \\\\nFig.\\\\xa04d) was acquired following k-fold cross-validation. We randomly \\\\nsplit all trials into tenfolds. we used ninefolds as training trials to com-\\\\npute d\\u2032late vs early. Trial-by-trial activity for the remaining onefold (test \\\\ntrials) was computed by averaging across the reward-prediction neu-\\\\nrons that met the selection criteria. We repeated this ten times until \\\\naverage population activity for every fold (and thus every trial) was \\\\nacquired.\\\\nT o obtain reward-prediction activity aligned to the first lick \\\\n(Fig.\\\\xa04j), only rewarded trials (leaf1) with a first lick happening after \\\\n2\\\\u2009m from corridor entry were included to enable us to investigate the \\\\nreward-prediction signal before licking starts.
